<header>Epistle to the</header>

...mysteries <note>The word begins on the previous page as "uner-" and completes here as "forschliche," meaning inscrutable or unfathomable.</note> of your Society. Thus far, people have not been successful in their discoveries, and by all appearances, the future will not exclude your lodges <term>Logen: the meeting places or organized branches of a fraternal society</term> from those knocking at your door as strangers <term>Fremdenklopfenden: literally "stranger-knockers," a term for uninitiated outsiders or those seeking entry into a secret society</term>. It is no wonder that conjectures in this regard have been very fertile. That which is hidden always excites curiosity, and this impulse, which sustains the realm of opinions, becomes unbearable when one's desire is not immediately satisfied.

Prejudices commonly accompany this impulse, and then—through its secret poison—it gives birth to envy, hatred, belittlement, mockery, and the harshest of accusations. These sorrowful effects have been observed in all ages whenever people have imagined themselves to be in quiet possession of truth and goodness. The weapons of malice and stupidity constantly unite whenever prejudice, ignorance, and hypocrisy are threatened with downfall. These children of darkness recognize their common origin and utility, and therefore it is no wonder that they [exert] their...

# Epistle to the

James 3:17 <note>original: "Jacob. 3. 17." This refers to the Epistle of James in the New Testament.</note>. This is the wisdom that I seek and honor with struggling effort; according to whose instruction I strive to live, and whose guidance I use in the judgment of my fellow human beings. 
I recognize from my own experience that we all fail in manifold ways, and for that reason the shouting of the great multitude makes me all the more attentive. I know that the people who shout "Hosanna" <note>original: "Hosianna." A Hebrew cry of praise or appeal for delivery; here referring to the crowd that welcomed Jesus to Jerusalem.</note> can be brought to the cry: "Crucify him!" <note>original: "Creutzige ihn."</note> through deceptive words. I am as little in a hurry to join in the shouting and follow the crowd as I am to reject and censure others. Believing too much and believing too little are both sources of sorrowful effects. Would we not rightly laugh at someone who tried to persuade us that the figure presented to us by his magnifying or diminishing glass <note>A metaphor for the lenses of prejudice or bias that distort the "size" or importance of a person's character.</note> was actually as large or as small as it appeared through the lens? If only unbelief and superstition would grasp this! A quiet contemplation of humanity and the knowledge of oneself teaches us the rule: test everything, and keep the best <note>A reference to 1 Thessalonians 5:21, a cornerstone of Enlightenment thought.</note>. With such a frame of mind, I have the

...heard and read reports about you, yet withheld my approval from most of them: for the childish, tasteless, and irrational elements were too obvious. The triangular customs <note>original: "dreyeckigte Gebräuche." A reference to the heavy use of the triangle as a symbol for the Deity and geometric perfection in Masonic ritual.</note>, the shoe transformed into a slipper <note>In certain Masonic initiation rites, the candidate is "neither barefoot nor shod" by wearing a slipper on one foot to symbolize humility.</note>, the measured pace of the new brother walking back and forth, the foot suspended in the air, the mysterious **J** and **B** <note>original: "J und B." These letters stand for Jachin and Boaz, the two pillars at the entrance of King Solomon’s Temple, central to Masonic symbolism.</note> were to me too Kabbalistic <term>Kabbalistic: relating to Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah); often used in the 18th century to dismiss rituals as occult or superstitious.</term>, too magic-like and too absurd, and therefore something I rejected as worthless. I held back my thoughts and hoped for better news. My attention, which has hitherto been dedicated to your Order, was greatly increased when I learned from public and secret reports of the establishment of a new lodge in Berlin. My affection for my native city and love for my fatherland mixed with zeal for what is true, good, and decent, and animated my effort to know your Order more closely and, if possible, to gather some certainty regarding its conduct and intentions. ->### Missive to the<-

these judgmental questions: Should one tolerate a society in Berlin without sufficient knowledge of its inner value? Should the great monarch reigning there, who wields the scepter with wisdom, reason, and magnanimity <note>A reference to Frederick the Great (Frederick II), who was himself a Freemason, though this was not always publicly discussed in the early years of his reign.</note>, should he protect an assembly of insolent and lecherous souls in the heart of his empire? I immediately inferred the conditional impossibility <note>The author concludes that since the King is wise and good, the rumors of Masonic depravity must be false.</note> and formed in my mind a favorable depiction of their meeting rooms. Finally, I received from our bookstore a collection of various works printed in Frankfurt am Main (perhaps Berlin), 1743, in octavo <note>original: "in 8." refers to octavo, a standard book size where the printed sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note>, brought to light for the benefit of your venerable society—

## Freemasonic Lodge in Berlin.

remarkable defensive writings, speeches, and other apologies <term>apologies: derived from the Greek "apologia," meaning a formal justification or defense of a belief or practice.</term> which have come to light. I read these with a sincere desire. My longing sought nourishment for my soul, which earnestly strives to find the means to distinguish the true from the false. After I had carefully perused this collection, I was—with much pleasure—convinced of the laudable qualities of their society and moved by the utility of the same. I must nevertheless confess that despite this favorable disposition, I must count myself among those who have chosen the middle path <note>original: "Mittelstrasse." This refers to a moderate, neutral stance, avoiding the extremes of blind hatred or blind devotion.</note> in judging your entire institution. You yourself, according to your own insight, will praise my conduct; for to simply approve of an Order which one knows—on account of its secrets <note>original: "Heimlichkeiten." I must explain myself more clearly. To approve—

<meta>This page continues the sentence from the bottom of page 11, which ended with the word "approve" (Billigen). This page begins by defining what it means to "approve" in a philosophical sense, continuing the author's cautious, rationalist approach to evaluating the Freemasons.</meta>

# Epistle to the
### Freemasonic Lodge in Berlin

means to affirm something as good. This cannot happen without knowledge <term>Erkenntniß: in Enlightenment philosophy, this refers to a clear, rational cognition or realization based on evidence.</term> of both its internal and external goodness; since one does not know everything that is specific to the Order, and consequently cannot perceive its internal goodness, no prudent person can demand from anyone a complete approval of the Order. 

I therefore make a well-founded distinction: between approving of the Order in its entirety, and approving of it in certain parts. I am duty-bound to do the latter, for I must recognize the good everywhere as good and grant my approval; the former cannot be required of me, nor of anyone, because one cannot assign a predicate <term>predicate: a term from formal logic referring to a quality or property attributed to a subject. The author argues that "good" is a predicate that cannot be logically applied to something unknown.</term> to that of which one has no mental representation <note>original: "Vorstellung." This refers to the concept or idea of a thing in the mind.</note>, and therefore one cannot attribute goodness to it, and thus not to the Order either. 

I am well aware of what the clever defenders of your assemblies say in order to obtain this approval. They say: "An Order that has such sublime, rational, and upright members must necessarily be good in all its institutions and correct in its principles <term>Grundſätzen: the fundamental laws or moral axioms of the society.</term>." Allow me, rational—

<summary>The author argues that total approval of the Freemasons is logically impossible for an outsider, as one cannot judge the "internal goodness" of a secret society without knowing its hidden nature. He challenges the defense that the high moral character of individual members automatically proves the goodness of the entire organization's principles.</summary>

->Freemasonic Lodge in Berlin.<-

—al gentlemen! that I may open my thoughts to you regarding this matter. The answer rests upon authority <note>original: "Ansehen." In this context, it refers to the prestige, reputation, or social standing of the Lodge members rather than objective evidence.</note>. In the realm of truth, this ground of proof falls away. It provides some probability, but no certainty. Where one asks for reasons, this way of proving something is not acceptable. The followers of the Roman Bishop <note>A common Enlightenment-era reference to the Pope and the Catholic Church, often used by Protestant writers to critique blind obedience to tradition over individual reason.</note> make exactly this same conclusion, and that is why so many remain on their paths of error. Reason and Scripture harmoniously reject this means of establishing certainty. "Judge not according to authority, but judge a righteous judgment," is the instruction of the latter <note>Referring to the Holy Scripture; specifically a paraphrase of John 7:24.</note>. I have therefore not been able to resolve to conclude in such a way, although I have the greatest reverence for your worthy Lodge.

While I cannot, in general, approve of everything about your Order, I do not therefore condemn it. I harbor the greatest loathing for those kinds of people who immediately pick up stones to kill their neighbor. One must never judge before the proper time. A ration—

### Epistle to the

—al doubt is the most certain means against superstition and unbelief, against hastiness and wild zeal. This [doubt] examines, listens, and allows itself to be instructed. Integrity <note>original: "Redlichkeit"; implying moral uprightness and sincerity of character.</note> cannot act against the truth, but only for the truth. It hates servile imitation and blind applause just as much as defiant opposition and arrogant self-righteousness. Such, my gentlemen, is the nature of my mind, which now has the honor of speaking with you. I give my approval to many of the principles that your secret Order <note>original: "verborgener Orden"; referring to the Freemasons’ practice of keeping their rituals and specific teachings hidden from the uninitiated.</note> reveals through its effects. The image I have formed most vividly of you possesses many of the most pleasant features, postures, and limbs. I cannot possibly be against you in this, even if I were to suffer the reproach, slander, or perhaps even persecution of certain heated souls for it. ### Open Letter to the

No one but you knows yourselves and is aware of your organization and laws; and therefore, I can reveal my doubts to no one else but you. I would have acted imprudently if I had shouted "Rebellion! Rebellion!" among others, and accompanied my doubts with sighs of "Ah!" and "Woe!" The thoughtless, the simple-minded, and the malicious would have used this to your disadvantage. Refutations that weep, lament, and call upon heaven <note>original: "den Himmel anrufende Wiederlegungen"; refers to overly emotional or religious rhetorical styles that prioritize drama over logic.</note> may stun, but they do not instruct or explain. Praise God! That our times are casting off and breaking this intolerable yoke. I cannot bear these envious ways of quarreling with one’s neighbor. They only sustain a swarm of prejudices. The thoughtless person accepts them immediately as certain truth and breaks the staff of condemnation <note>original: "bricht den Stab der Verdammung"; a historical German legal custom where a judge broke a wooden staff over a prisoner to signify that a death sentence was final and the person's life was forfeit.</note> without sufficient cause; the simple-minded person accompanies them with a sigh over these evil times and the judgments of God; and the malicious person only learns to increase the number of "ists" to be rejected <note>original: "Isten"; the author is referring to the common 18th-century practice of labeling opponents with suffixes like "Atheists," "Deists," or "Spinozists" to dismiss their arguments.</note>. This is what it means to treat one's neighbor in a godless manner. So that sincerity

The nature of Freemasonry could find no more recognizable outline of its social final purpose <term>Entzweck: the ultimate goal or intended end-result of a society's existence.</term> than this (§. 1); therefore, it has chosen this name for its designation, in order to represent its nature in general through this sign. However, so that it might distinguish itself from this purely mechanical trade and thus define itself correctly, it has added an explanatory adjective to the word Mason. This adjective **Free** reveals to us that in this society, one employs the powers of the intellect to choose what is best. ->§. 3.<-

<margin>Origin of the Order.</margin>
This Order is both old and new: for although this society is said to have taken its beginning during the building of Kensington Palace <note>The author refers to Kensington Palace in London; early Masonic histories often linked the craft to specific royal building projects to claim antiquity.</note> and given itself the name "Freemasons," it has nonetheless existed since the time of the creation of the angels, the world, and mankind, and all these bodies are made from Freemasonic qualities.

->Circular letter to the<-

[The reception] of a new brother occurs with special ceremonies, which I hesitate to recount because they do not all seem probable to me as they are found written down. This much is certain: a forceful oath <note>original: "nachdrüklicher Eyd." These oaths typically involved swearing secrecy under the threat of symbolic penalties.</note> must be taken by the brother to be initiated; that a sword-bearer with a bared sword is present; and that the first entry causes terror, horror, and dread. This latter arrangement was perhaps borrowed by the Israelite Freemasons <note>The author is likely referring to the heavy influence of Old Testament themes and Solomon’s Temple in Masonic ritual, or the historical presence of Jewish members in certain early lodges.</note> from the thunder and lightning at Mount Sinai and shared with their uncircumcised <note>A term used here to describe the non-Jewish or "Gentile" members of the fraternity.</note> brothers. 

Upon admission into the society, one must pay a certain sum of money into the common treasury. This depends on the arrangement of each lodge. 
<margin>p. 169.</margin> In Berlin, it is 50 Reichsthaler <term>Reichsthaler: A large silver coin used in the German states. 50 Reichsthaler was a substantial amount, roughly equivalent to several months' wages for a skilled laborer, suggesting the lodge was quite exclusive.</term>. In Hamburg, it is 30 Reichsthaler, and in others, 3 guineas <term>Guinea: A British gold coin; its mention highlights the international connection between German and English Freemasonry.</term>, as the entry fee. It is not improbable that one contributes something monthly, or at every meeting, to increase the treasury. 

<margin>p. 111.</margin> The funds that have already been collected in Berlin; the [property] <note>The text breaks here at the syllable "Ver-", likely continuing as "Versammlungshaus" (meeting house) or "Vermögen" (assets) on the following page.</note> purchased there...

### Epistle to the

St. John’s Day <term>St. John’s Day: The feast of St. John the Baptist (June 24) is the traditional high holiday for Freemasonry, marking the summer solstice and the anniversary of the founding of the first Grand Lodge in 1717.</term> is the most distinguished feast day of the Order. On this day, the Order—where its lodges have the freedom to do so—holds a public, solemn procession. One enjoys oneself on this day with all manner of sensory and physical delights; yet the poor are not forgotten; and philanthropy <note>original: "Menschenliebe." In the 18th century, this term carried a strong Enlightenment connotation of active, practical service to humanity.</note> proves itself very busy on this day, even outside the Order. Regular meetings occur twice or even three times a week. In the winter, these last from 5:00 to 8:00, and 
<margin>p. 108.</margin>
in the summer from 7:00 to 10:00. In these gatherings, one observes the order according to the time of admission; therefore, it sometimes happens that a merchant, an artist, and so forth, sits above a Duke, a Peer, or even indeed a Prince. Within the lodge, one speaks in known languages and no "thieves' cant" <term>thieves’ cant: The original "rothwelsche Sprache" refers to a secret jargon or slang used by vagrants and criminals in early modern Germany. The author emphasizes that Masons use clear, standard language to distance them from suspicions of illicit activity.</term> or invented language. 

The Order has a Treasurer and a Secret Secretary <note>original: "geheimen Schreiber"</note> who keeps the archive in
<margin>p. 108. 109.</margin>
order, records the new members, and collects that which the busy members present. This is a treasure of the sciences and arts—

### Epistle to the...

**can** be more laudable than this endeavor, to distance oneself from the tyranny and error of the world, and to seek a sanctuary under the wings of truth and freedom? Prejudices find no residence in their dwellings, and the word of a master is not met with applause simply because the master has said it, but rather because it convinces through its own weight. One assembles in order to recognize—without conceit or prejudice—<margin>p. 95.</margin> the fundamental causes of things through correct concepts of understanding, and to strengthen this through convincing experience. Everything is investigated according to the utmost rigor. Erudition and science do not dwell here amidst stubbornness, bickering, envy, and jealousy, as they do in the high schools <note>original: "hohen Schulen," referring to universities or traditional academic institutions of the time, which the author views as plagued by petty rivalry.</note>. Here there are none other than brothers, comrades, and friends, who banish pedantic <term>schulfüchsig: A term for narrow-minded, overly formal, or "school-masterly" behavior that focuses on rigid rules rather than actual wisdom.</term> and stubborn ways; they revere the truth with a cheerful and enlightened countenance and seek its growth. <meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous page, shifting from the "Stoic" view of the body as a prison to the author's own view of the body as a necessary and divine component of human existence.</meta>

->### Freemason Lodge in Berlin.<-

A human being without a body is a contradiction. If I take the body away from myself, then I am only soul. For that reason, I do not complain about these bonds. My body does not chain me to the earth on its own; it only does so when my spirit becomes earthly-minded. Through the body, my sensory perceptions <note>original: "Sinnlichkeiten," referring to the inputs of the five senses as a means of experiencing the world.</note> raise me toward heaven, and its instruments <note>The physical organs and senses.</note> make me a wise admirer of the Creator within His creation <note>original: "im Geschöpfe," literally "within the creature," referring to the divine workmanship evident in the human form.</note>. I provide it with its food so that its physical composition might be maintained. Its well-being delights me, and through its artistic structure <note>A common 18th-century metaphor for the complex "architecture" of human anatomy.</note>, I am encouraged to praise Him who assigned this—albeit fragile—hut to me as a dwelling place.

Every creature is good and not harmful if it is received with thanksgiving. A strict Carthusian <note>A monk of a Catholic order known for extreme silence, solitude, and fasting.</note> is as outlandish to me as a knight-errant, and a doctrine that turns the abstaining from certain foods into a trade of godliness is just as wretched to me as that one is ridiculous which says to me: I fast twice a week and eat fish and [baked...] <note>The sentence continues onto the next page with the word "Gebackenes" (baked goods).</note>

# Epistle to the

...baked goods. Everything that the goodness of the Creator has made is very good. Why should one not grant the body its due honor? Freemasonry is to be praised for seeking the body's care in order to thereby encourage the spirit. A miser, unbearable to humanity, and a simple-minded hermit disguise and disfigure these privileges of communal life. Scripture and Reason reject their gaunt image and surly system of doctrine. The intention which the Lodges show in this matter is beyond reproach; only the motivation for it is not to be approved in all cases.

One must by no means confuse the Festivals of Bacchus <note>original: "Bachusfeste"; these were ancient Greek and Roman festivals in honor of the god of wine, often characterized by wild intoxication and excess.</note> of the ancient Greeks and Romans with the ritual meals of the Freemasons. The former sought, by filling the body with delicious foods and various drinks, the lulling of the passions to the detriment of good morals; the latter, however, seek the inspiration of the mental faculties <term>Gemüthskräfte: A central concept in 18th-century German thought referring to the combined powers of the mind, soul, and emotions.</term> through the arrangement of a sensory pleasure in well-prepared dishes or pleasant wines. In short: sensory pleasure is, for them—

### Circular Letter to the

—an <note>original: "nen", completing "einen" (an) from the previous page's "Every member has a—"</note> entrance into all lodges in the world. However, he must be a member. But how could one brother reveal himself to another, both inside and outside the meeting rooms, in a foreign land and in a place whose language he does not understand, if there were not a language commonly used throughout the Order? How could one distinguish a fake <note>original: "verstellten"; meaning disguised or dissimulating.</note> brother from a true one with certainty without sufficient marks? This sign language <term>Zeichensprache: The system of gestures, grips, and passwords used by Freemasons for mutual recognition.</term> is a sufficient means to achieve both intentions. Experience confirms all of this. And these hidden expressions have this advantage: that they can be learned in a short time, and they are a particular benefit for becoming acquainted with a great number of honorable <term>rechtschaffener: A term from the Enlightenment referring to people of integrity, honesty, and moral uprightness.</term> people.

those degrees of obligations that arise from general, specific, and very specific motives. It <note>referring to "rational philanthropy" from the previous page</note> treads with a convincing prudence <term>Klugheit: A key Enlightenment virtue meaning practical wisdom or the ability to apply moral principles to real-world situations.</term> the divided steps of its dutiful conduct when it breaks bread with its neighbor. The prudent Order of Freemasons follows this wise division of the duties of love. In common life, it loves without restriction. Its love, when it is among the inhabitants of the earth, produces social intercourse, courtesy, friendship, compassion, assistance, help, and rescue. If it is among the brothers, then this love incites it toward union, and creates intimate fellowship, heartfelt mercy, like-mindedness, and tenderness. The Lodges consider themselves bound to all people, <margin>p. 144.</margin> but nevertheless to those who are of their own nation and country more than to foreigners; to those who are known to them more than to mere strangers; to those who are their intimates more than to mere acquaintances; and to their relatives and blood friends— <note>The text here describes a "hierarchy of duties," a common Enlightenment ethical concept where one has a universal duty to all humans, but a more immediate or "special" duty to those closer to them (family, friends, and fellow citizens).</note>

to those, who are even more than accepted friends, by showing them love. A fair division! Following this zeal for love, this Order seeks to promote its preservation and growth in general, and for every member in particular, by all lawful means. A sick fellow brother, who is unable to afford the necessities for his care, is nursed. One burdened with debts—provided there is testimony of his honesty <term>Redlichkeit: a core Enlightenment virtue implying integrity, sincerity, and moral uprightness</term>—is helped out of his distressed circumstances by the payment of his debts. One bowed down by unexpected misfortune finds refuge in the help of his fellow brothers; in short: the efforts of the members seek to create the well-being <term>Glückseeligkeit: a central Enlightenment term for a state of happiness or "blessedness" achieved through virtuous living and social harmony</term> of every individual through united care.

# Missive to the

it recognizes the weakness of our powers, in the finitude and limitation of its members both within and outside of the lodges. Nevertheless, its utility is palpable, and thus its preservation is necessary, because it labors to gradually improve—in a human way—that which is not good all at once, and yet is capable of being improved. <note>The author argues that while human nature is flawed, the Order provides a structured process for incremental moral progress.</note> It seeks to make its members—through their incorporation into its institution—into civilized human beings, content inhabitants of the earth, and well-behaved citizens. Perfection is one thing; the striving toward it is quite another. The latter is the <term>mainspring: the "Treibfeder" or primary motive force</term> of its actions, and the former is their ultimate goal. A dilapidated building can never become a new one through wit, intellect, <note>original: "Wiz" — in the 18th century, this referred to cleverness or ingenious spirit rather than modern humor.</note> art, diligence, and care; however, one can make it habitable and useful through repair, the clearing away of rubble and filth, and through decoration. Is this not necessary and useful? What rational person would dispute the value of the Order, which works toward such an end with regard to humanity?

### §. 7.

<margin>
Certain fundamental laws of the Order, by which one may also recognize the character of those admitted into it.
</margin>

The defenders of the Freemasons have spared no effort to clear the Order of the charge that it is a hindrance or harmful to Christianity. For this reason, they have on every occasion disclosed their fundamental laws—which do not actually pertain to their secrets—and at the same time made known the character of those who can and cannot become members of this Order. I will briefly recount those laws I have noted, so that a clearer conception of this assembly may be formed.

<margin>
p. 173.
II.
</margin>

I. Atheists and Deists are not admitted or tolerated within the Order. They are regarded as vermin <note>The original German "Ungeziefer" (vermin or pests) illustrates the harsh 18th-century social and legal rejection of those who denied the existence of a personal God or divine revelation.</note> who do as little good for the secular state as they do for the spiritual one. A candidate who holds even a single opinion that leads toward Deism or Atheism—

<meta>catchword: —ism</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental laws of the Masonic Order in Berlin, specifically addressing the requirement that members must not be atheists or deists, defending the Order against claims that it is anti-Christian.</summary>

62   Missive to the

<note>original: "Atheisterey," an archaic, derogatory term for atheism</note> ry leads, and allows itself to be perceived, he will be rejected.

<margin>p. 11.</margin>
II. A person who, for many years, neglects to publicly attend **divine service** <note>The German "Gottesdienst" refers to formal church services; 18th-century society viewed church attendance as a primary indicator of moral and civic reliability.</note> according to the customs of the congregation in which he was baptized, shall be excluded; consequently, a despiser of the public worship of Christians cannot become a **Freemason**.

<margin>* p. 12.</margin>
III. The Order admits only **Christians** into its fellowship, and all Christian denominations <note>original: "Gemeinen," referring here to different church bodies or confessions.</note> have, without distinction, a right of entry into its assemblies. Outside of the Christian Church, no Freemason can or may be accepted. Therefore, **Jews, Muslims**, <note>original: "Mahometaner," a historical term for Muslims.</note> and **Heathens**, <note>original: "Heyden," referring to non-monotheistic or non-Abrahamic peoples.</note> as unbelievers, are excluded from it. How often does a man loathe his own past actions—unless he has not the slightest impression of humanity left within him. Shall a man, who now becomes a murderer, a thief, or an unjust person, be said to have a good and innocent nature? Is he supposed to be pleased with himself in these deeds? He must, after all, have willed them before he accomplished them, and yet after their completion, he no longer wills them. Wherein lies the <term>sufficient reason</term> <note>German: *zureichender Grund*. If so, he must indeed find something evil within himself. Can one seek this evil anywhere else but in nature? Who would want to attribute such things to the clever <term>Freemasons</term>? Who would believe that they were <term>Naturalists</term> in this way?

# Circular letter to the #

But if a **Naturalist** is one who serves God according to <term>natural religion</term> <note>A popular Enlightenment concept suggesting that religious truths can be discovered through reason and observation of nature, independent of organized religion or supernatural revelation.</note>; whose Christianity is as old as the world <note>A reference to the famous Deist book *Christianity as Old as the Creation* (1730) by Matthew Tindal, which argued that the essence of religion is fixed in human reason.</note>; who, as a philosopher, strives to practice the law of nature; who does not reject revelation, but neither does he [entirely] accept it; who loves as a human being; who is obliging as a friend; who fulfills his duties to his superiors as a citizen; whose heart is honest, whose social interaction is affectionate, whose intentions are virtuous, and whose conduct is blameless; who loves, honors, protects, and seeks virtue, truth, and freedom; who applies himself to innocence; who strives to be useful to everyone; and who considers every day lost in which he has not done something good; if this is a **Naturalist**, and this **Naturalist** is a **Freemason**—or if the **Freemason** admits such men into his **Lodge** and among his brothers—is this wrong? Can one find fault with this sketched portrait of a **Freemason**? # Epistle to the #

If the Freemasons are such naturalists <term>Naturalists: In the 18th century, this referred to adherents of "Natural Religion," who believed morality and knowledge of God could be achieved through human reason and nature alone, without the need for biblical revelation.</term>, then this must be the most blessed society, and it would then also be true that man is not as wretched and evil as he is depicted to us. Yet this they cannot be, for these naturalists belong to the realm of possibilities, but not to our current real world. In a verse, a note, or a draft, they may well be constrained and depicted according to the letter of Monsieur Chaulieu <note>Guillaume Amfrye de Chaulieu (1639–1720), the French poet whose work was quoted on the previous page.</note>; but to represent them in reality and in fact—that is what ties the knot of doubt. Such naturalists do not exist at all, and they are absolutely impossible in this present world. In isolation, I can describe them. Moral philosophy <term>Sittenlehre: The study of ethics and the principles of right conduct.</term> thus designs them according to the law of nature; but only on paper. # Epistle to the
<note>original: "Sendschreiben an die"—this refers to the letter addressed to the Freemason Lodge mentioned on the previous page.</note>

I know of no other word than the word **Naturalist**. Accordingly, wherever there is a **Naturalist**, there is a rational being who possesses a **proficiency** <term>proficiency: translating "Fertigkeit," which in 18th-century philosophy refers to a "habitus"—a stable disposition or skill acquired through practice that allows one to perform virtuous acts easily.</term> in natural godliness. A proficiency presupposes an ease in making real—through one’s internal and external effort—that which is naturally and morally possible. It includes within itself a constant practice of godliness and permits no deviation from the recognized **service of God** <note>original: "Dienste Gottes"—referring to worship or religious duty.</note>. From this, I draw these conclusions:

**Firstly.** Without a correct knowledge of God, and of that which is in and through God, one is no Naturalist.

**Secondly.** Without a proficiency in leading a natural, divine **way of life** <note>original: "Wandel"—referring to one's general conduct and moral path.</note>, one is no Naturalist.

**Thirdly.** Whoever wishes to be a Naturalist must serve God perfectly in natural religion; for an imperfect service of God is no service of God at all. The shadow is not the light,

->Epistle to the<-

Is it right, then, to immediately turn the doubters, the scoffers of **Revelation**, or those who will accept nothing without proof, into **Naturalists**? No! The title is far too sublime for those who only speak of the **laws of nature** and harbor a good opinion of themselves and their conduct. The name of **Naturalist** belongs alone to him whom the **Creator** first formed upright and who, without compulsion, honors His commands in a state of constant rectitude. Certainly a **Socrates**, **Antoninus**, and **Plato** <note>The author refers to the classical philosophers Socrates, Marcus Aurelius Antoninus, and Plato, often cited by Enlightenment thinkers as "virtuous pagans" who lived morally through reason alone.</note> deserve this name as little as a **Spinoza**, **Tindal**, and **Whiston** <note>Baruch Spinoza, Matthew Tindal, and William Whiston were controversial 17th- and 18th-century thinkers associated with Pantheism and Deism, often accused by the orthodox of undermining divine revelation.</note>. A beautiful garment often covers an unhealthy body; it is the internal, not the external, that defines the **Naturalist**.

I do not wish to deny these people their external respectability, nor certain strokes of the image of a **Naturalist**. However, I deny for sufficient reasons that they were actual **Naturalists**, and that any will ever be—

F 5

... [to be]. There is a noticeable difference between *being* a **Naturalist** and *wanting* to be one. The former I deny; the latter I admit. In all ages, people have been found who spoke and wrote rationally, well, and clearly about the **law of nature**, about **natural duties**, and about human actions. The follies of mankind have been depicted. But is a portrait an active person? Does not the fulfillment of truths belong to a truly rational human being? A child is already a human being, although it cannot yet walk, speak, or act rationally. But is it, for that reason, a **moral** <term>sittlicher: relating to "Sittlichkeit," which encompasses both morality and social ethics</term> human being? Mere limbs are not enough for that. **Morality** requires free, lawful actions. The moral human being is my **Naturalist**—not the one who is theoretically demonstrated, not the one who merely imagines it of himself, not the one who chatters about it, not the one who rejects **Revelation**, and not the one who sacrifices himself to the merely sensual. I like to [dwell] upon the natural and moral...

<meta>This page precedes the previously translated section. ### Missive to the

Is one acting rightly when one immediately turns the doubters, the mockers of **revelation**, or those who refuse to accept anything without scrutiny, into **Naturalists** <term>Naturalisten: in this 18th-century context, a "Naturalist" refers to someone who believes that religious and moral truths can be derived from nature and reason alone, often excluding divine revelation</term>? No! This title is far too lofty for those who merely speak of the **law of nature** and harbor a high opinion of themselves and their conduct. The name of "Naturalist" belongs alone to him whom the **Creator** first formed uprightly, and who, without coercion, obediently honors His commands in constant rectitude. Certainly, a **Socrates**, **Antoninus** <note>original: "Antonin"; likely referring to the Roman Emperor and Stoic philosopher Marcus Aurelius Antoninus</note>, and **Plato** deserve the name just as little as a **Spinoza**, **Tindal** <note>Matthew Tindal, an influential English Deist</note>, and **Whiston** <note>William Whiston, a theologian and mathematician known for his unorthodox religious views</note>. A beautiful garment often covers an unhealthy body; it is the internal, not the external, that constitutes the Naturalist.

I do not wish to deny these people external respectability and certain traits of the image of a Naturalist. However, I deny for sufficient reasons that they were actual Naturalists, and that there will ever be any who...

# Freemason Lodge in Berlin.

...become. There is a notable difference between *being* a **Naturalist** and *wanting* to be one. I deny the former, but I admit the latter. In all times, people have been found who spoke and wrote reasonably, well, and clearly about the **law of nature**, about natural duties, and about human actions. They have depicted the follies of mankind. Is a painting, then, an active person? Does not the fulfillment of truths belong to a truly rational human being? A child is already a human, even if it cannot yet walk, speak, or act rationally. But is it therefore a **moral human** <term>sittlicher Mensch: a person whose character is defined by ethical maturity and the conscious practice of virtue</term>? Mere limbs are not enough for that. **Morality** requires free, lawful actions. The moral human is my Naturalist—not the one who is merely portrayed, not the one who imagines it of himself, not the one who chatters about it, not the one who rejects **revelation** <note>original: "Offenbahrung"; the author is distinguishing his "Naturalist" from the typical Enlightenment skeptic who dismisses divine scripture</note>, and not the one who sacrifices himself to the purely sensual. I may find myself at home with the natural and moral...

# Circular Letter to the...

...wretched times! Terrible **abomination of desolation**! <note>original: *Greuel der Verwüstung*. A biblical reference (Matthew 24:15) used here to describe the spiritual ruin caused by religious infighting and hypocrisy.</note> How long will this mischief prevail? These two wretched mothers of the misbegotten births of religions hide their ill-mannered conduct, when one opposes their mischief, under the phrase **zeal for religion**. No! This is not the zeal that true religion demands; rather, it is a wild wordplay of raging passions and a disguise of the holy, which instead lies within those calm impulses—the effective and amiable effort of true worship. 

How blessed is he who is not offended by these monsters! My melancholy has perhaps spoken too candidly here. Reason, truth, and godliness will justify me. <header>Epistle to the</header>

ther? <note>The text continues from the previous page: "to what end would the law otherwise be necessary?"</note> Does the law then leave to my freedom the proposition: "I will do what I want"? No! It restricts my willing; it says: choose that which is **lawful**, for that is the best. How then does **freedom of conscience** <term>Gewissensfreyheit: the Enlightenment-era concept that one's inner moral judgment should be free from state or church coercion, provided it is guided by reason</term> act? It seizes upon the consequent will that arises from conviction. The conscience is thereafter instructed, advised, and guided toward the best. It learns to examine itself. Its judgments become the fundamental rules of **moral actions**. According to these, everything is either willed or not willed. It seeks what is decent; it seizes the best, and makes those whom it governs into conscientious people.

Do people actually act this way? Very few indeed. Can I truly grant freedom of conscience to one who acts against himself out of a conscience that is erroneous, incorrect, and clouded by **prejudice**? VI. From those principles of the Freemasonic Order already made known, the following flows naturally: One must be a faithful subject in every state where one lives, and recognize the right of the Prince <term>Fürsten: a general term for a sovereign ruler or monarch</term> and the regular authorities <term>Obrigkeit: the established government or magistracy</term> over oneself. I do not believe that any rational person would criticize the conduct of the Freemasons according to this rule. They speak of the high authorities with the greatest reverence. They call such power one originating from God <note>original: "von GOtt herrührende Gewalt"</note> and attribute to the regents an image of that Being which governs all things, and for whose sake one must be subject to them. The endeavor of the Order is always directed toward having members who are faithful and zealous defenders of the highest power, and especially submissive subjects of the Prince, whose born or acquired subjects they may be. Therefore, they banish-

So that all suspicion may be removed from their meeting rooms <note>referring to Masonic Lodges</note>, which are so loyal to the authorities <term>Obrigkeit: the established government or legal power</term>, one of the members has drafted a treatise on the rights of a prince <note>original: "Fürst," a sovereign ruler of a territory</note> in regard to the Freemasons, and has placed a short but forceful <margin>p. 181.</margin> discourse on the subject into the collection for the Book of Constitutions <note>the fundamental rules and history of a Masonic jurisdiction</note>. equity, and prudence. However, this restriction seems to nullify the former point: for an absolute power that does not look toward the law of nature, equity, and prudence, is a tyranny.

Can a prince banish his loyal subjects from the land solely because they are Freemasons, when he has in no way forbidden them from entering into this Order? **No!** Because a subject does not deserve such punishments until he has broken the laws through action or omission <note>original: "Thun oder Lassen," a legal concept referring to both things done and things left undone</note>. **No!** The proof shall be this: a prince cannot legally command a revelation of thoughts, unless it were of particular importance to the common good <term>gemeinen Wesen: the public welfare, state, or commonwealth</term>. Through the compact <note>original: "Vergleich," referring here to a formal agreement or contract</note> that has been established between the **Master of the Order** <term>Ordensmeister: the presiding officer or Grand Master who governs the lodge and its members</term> and the member being admitted, the entire **Freemason Order** acquires a well-founded right. By virtue of this right, they can compel such a member to observe the **secrecy** <term>Verschwiegenheit: the solemn obligation to maintain silence regarding the Order’s internal rituals and mysteries</term> promised under oath. And the **Prince** <note>The "Prince" (Fürst) refers to the sovereign ruler of the state</note> is in no way able to infringe upon this well-acquired right of the entire Order. He would not even be able to do so if only a valid promise had been made, let alone when such a promise has been confirmed by the most powerful **oath**.

<margin>p. 185.</margin>
Can a private individual, without the prior knowledge of the **authorities**, induce another to promise secrecy with an **oath**? Yes! I maintain that the following proof has not been correctly determined for this question, namely: that everything is permitted which is not prohibited by **natural** or **civil law** <note>Natural law refers to universal moral principles, while civil law refers to the specific statutes of a nation</note>; and particularly within the **civil state**, one is free to enter into any and all contracts that are not contrary to **good morals** <note>original: "gute Sitten," a legal concept (bonos mores) meaning public decency or the shared moral standards of a community</note>, and to [confirm] such things by oath...

For all these cases refer to something that is lawful, and which the **sovereign** <note>original: "Landesherr," the ruling prince or lord of the territory</note>, because it is lawful, tacitly commands. This principle, however, cannot be applied to the secret of the Order for the simple reason that the sovereign does not know whether it is lawful. If the **authorities** <note>original: "Obrigkeit"</note> are to permit something because it is harmful to no man’s conscience, they must first [know] that which is supposed to be so...

VII. Although the Order <note>The "Order" refers to the Freemasons.</note> accepts all persons of the male gender as members, this does not happen without an examination of the person one wishes to admit. This term describes a combination of philanthropy and "affability"—a kind, approachable, and social disposition toward others.</term> must be practiced with respect and agreeableness. One must seek no personal merit in one's good deeds. Toward the suffering, the needy, and the unfortunate—to whose help, refreshment, and the lightening of their burden one has contributed—one must not let it be perceived that one desires great expressions of gratitude or high praise for these works of love. **The right hand must not know what the left hand gives** <note>A reference to the biblical teaching in Matthew 6:3, which instructs that charitable acts should be performed in secret rather than for public recognition.</note>, and one must not broadcast their favors by recounting what has been done. ### Freemason Lodge in Berlin.

A reasonable **kind-heartedness** <term>original: "Gutherzigkeit." A disposition toward doing good, but here emphasized as being "reasonable" or "rational" rather than purely emotional.</term> must be accompanied by wise reflection in the members of the Order; and therefore, <margin>p. 111.</margin> they are not moved by the pity that vicious and dissolute people <note>The text distinguishes between the "unfortunate" who deserve help and those whose suffering is a direct result of "lasterhafte" (vicious/immoral) or "liederliche" (dissolute/reckless) living.</note> arouse in weak souls.

<margin>p. 129.</margin>
**Sound morality** <term>original: "gesunde Sittlichkeit." This refers to a robust, practical ethics rather than just theoretical rules.</term> is required as a quality in the lodges of the Freemasons. This quality arises from the purpose of the founding of the Order. The Masonic Order was established to produce men—and indeed, agreeable men, good citizens, faithful subjects who keep their word inviolably, and faithful worshippers of the **God of friendship** <note>A common Enlightenment-era reference to the Divine, emphasizing the fraternal and social duties of religion over specific sectarian dogmas.</note>, who look more to virtue than to rewards. Yet, one does not bind oneself to mere civic virtues. The entire **philosophy of the mind** and the entire **theology of the heart** are understood under the concept of sound morality.

<margin>p. 132. 17.</margin> **Discretion** <term>original: "Verschwiegenheit." In Masonic context, this refers to the ability to keep the secrets of the Order and the private matters of the brethren.</term> is a primary characteristic of a Freemason. This great science is learned upon entry into the Order. It preserves the quintessence of the secrets, whereby <margin>p. 177.</margin> the society remains the best in the world, and through the violation of which it would become the worst. This is the primary advantage of the Order, by which the <margin>p. 51.</margin> internal workings of the lodges are protected and preserved from all disorder. Through this virtue, that which is reserved only for those who enter the society is withheld from the curious. Silence is the cloud that surrounds the entrance to this society, illuminates the sanctuary in this tabernacle, lays the foundation for the veneration of the Order, and makes it admirable in its unrecognized value.

<margin>p. 134.</margin> A taste for the useful sciences and **liberal arts** <term>original: "freyen Künsten." # Freemason Lodge in Berlin.

The **Order** requires of each of its members that he contribute something through his protection, generosity, or labor toward an extensive work for which no **Academy** <note>Referring to formal scientific institutions of the era, such as the Prussian Academy of Sciences or the Royal Society in London.</note> is sufficient. This is because all such societies consist of only a small number of people, and therefore their efforts cannot encompass a subject of such great scope. All Grand Masters in **Germany**, **England**, **Italy**, and other lands exhort all scholars and all artists of the brotherhood to collect, with united diligence, the materials for a **general dictionary of the liberal arts and useful sciences**, **theology** and **politics** <note>original: "Gottesgelahrtheit und Staatskunst." From its inception, modern Freemasonry forbade the discussion of religion and partisan politics within the lodge to ensure peace and fraternal harmony.</note> alone excepted.

A beginning has already been made on this work in **London**, and through the union of the brethren, it will be possible to bring it to its perfection in a few years. In this same work, not only shall the technical terms and their derivations be explained, but also the

# Epistle to the

...and lays the foundation for rumors and slander. However, one acts unreasonably if one immediately makes a negative judgment without first understanding what the **Order** <term>original: "Orden"; referring to the Masonic fraternity</term> means by it. So that everything may be recognized in an orderly fashion, I wish to reveal the secret just as my reflections have conceived it while contemplating the appendix to the **Book of Constitutions** <term>original: "Constitutionenbuch"; the governing rules and history of a Masonic Grand Lodge</term> of the **Freemasons**. The **Masonic secret** <term>original: "Freymäurergeheimniß"</term> is nothing other than: 
<margin>p. 132.</margin> the internal order that governs the society and is maintained through figurative 
<margin>p. 154.</margin> signs and specifically chosen words, both inside and outside the **Lodges** <term>local chapters or meeting places of the fraternity</term>, in order to recognize the **brethren** <term>original: "Mitbrüder"; fellow members</term> and to understand one another even at the greatest distance. This **definition of the word** <note>original: "Worterklärung"; a nominal definition explaining how the term is used</note> provides a clearly distinct concept of this term, and one complains unjustly when saying that one does not know the secret of the Freemasons. I am well aware that an **explanation of the matter itself** <note>original: "Sachenerklärung"; a real or essential definition that explains the actual nature and internal workings of the craft</note> is demanded; however, the Order denies this to everyone without exception who is not one of its members...

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<summary>The author defines the "Masonic secret" as a practical system of signs and passwords used for mutual recognition among members. He argues that while the public can understand the definition of the secret, the true essence of the fraternity's work remains reserved for initiated members.</summary>

The "Eleusinian Mysteries" were the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note> was served by secret societies; in **Egypt**, **Isis**; in **Athens**, **Minerva** <note>original: "Minerva"; the Roman goddess of wisdom, equivalent to the Greek Athena.</note>; among the **Phoenicians**, **Urania** <note>original: "Urania"; a title for the goddess Aphrodite meaning "Heavenly," associated with spiritual rather than physical love.</note>; and among the **Scythians**, **Diana** <note>original: "Diana"; the Roman goddess of the hunt, equivalent to the Greek Artemis.</note>. The **Freemasons** <term>original: "Freymäurer"</term> have imitated these, borrowed what was fitting, and gradually improved upon them. A thinking person can recognize them from their intentions (§. 5.); indeed, they even make some of them known themselves. ### §. 10.

<margin>
One must not form a hateful and terrible idea of the Freemasonry order.

# Sendschreiben an die
<meta>catchword: friend</meta>

...everyone will easily recognize from this treatise that I have examined the reasons which the <term>Order</term> <note>original: "Orden"; referring to the Freemasons.</note> gave to prove the <term>fairness</term> <note>original: "Billigkeit"; in this context, it implies the reasonableness, equity, or justice of the Order's rules and demands.</note> of its demands, and that I am not one of those who seek to slander. My doubts rest on various grounds. I shall list them.

A rational man desires nothing in any way other than how he perceives it to be good within its proper context. A wise man seeks sufficient, lawful, and the best means to realize his intention, and a prudent man does not employ them until reason reveals to him the superior quality of their goodness in comparison to other means. Can my will be moved before my understanding perceives what is glorious, useful, and worthy of veneration? Am I in a position to choose if I do not know the good?

My friend wishes to entice me and presents that which is meant to allure me under a **covered dish** <note>original: "verdeckten Schüssel"; a metaphor for the secrets of the Order being hidden from the initiate until after they have already committed to joining.</note>. A perverse arrangement! How am I to find pleasure in my prize while it is under the cover? I do not even know if my

### Freemason Lodge in Berlin. 181

...friend does not wish to pleasantly <term>deceive</term> <note>original: "berücken"; meaning to ensnare, charm, or delude.</note> me. Do I want the concealed prize? No! > A concealed prize indeed entices the sensual nature and stirs one’s desires; but a rational and wise man, if he does not wish to be deceived, must want and demand nothing other than what he recognizes as good and in accordance with his **perfections**.

How I should feel ashamed that I demanded something so contrary to the rules of **reason**, **wisdom**, and **prudence**. I withdraw into my silence and regret the deception of my senses. How do I maintain these active impulses? In no other way than through living knowledge

### Freemason Lodge in Berlin.
M 4
<meta>The catchword "Guten" (Good) at the bottom of the previous page connects to the discussion of following "the Good" on this page.</meta>

...can deny this proposition: A wise and rational man must at all times strive for a **clear understanding** of what he intends to choose, so that he is not deceived by his senses. To enter into a society such as that of the **Freemasons** is to accept a society that one does not know, to approve of its laws and regulations, and to confirm with an **oath** <note>original: "Eyde"; the secret oaths of Freemasonry were a major point of contention for 18th-century critics who believed one should never swear to obey unknown rules.</note> that what one does not yet know—and of which one has not understood the slightest bit—is good, right, and virtuous. Does this not mean overturning the **moral laws of the soul**? I am well aware that the sensory <note>original: "Sinnliche"; referring to the aspects of human nature driven by physical impulses and desires rather than the intellect.</note> often desires, and can desire, something without proper reasons, merely according to appearance. I have spoken of this already. The **maxim of Medea** <note>original: "Satz der Medeen"; a reference to the famous line from Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*: "I see the better and approve it, but I follow the worse." The author argues this moral failing applies only to those enslaved by their passions.</note> remains true only for deceitful desires when they lead their slaves, and not in the consideration of rational men. The latter recognize and follow that which is **good**; the former see what is best as long as they investigate wisely and according to **Reason**, but through the sight of an apparent—

<header>Epistle to the</header>

<page-num>196</page-num>

...really make [me] perfect? <note>This sentence completes the question from the previous page: "Will a lie really make [me] perfect?"</note> Can it truly improve my condition in reality? Whatever is untrue is imperfect, for it contradicts itself and negates itself, and can never, by its own nature, make anything good in eternity. I conclude from this: I cannot determine the goodness of a thing without recognizing its truth; and if I wish to say, "this thing is good," then I must recognize its truth and know *why* it is good. **Conscience** judges actions insofar as they have been determined according to the truth—toward the choice of the good—or have been directed toward the non-good through deviation. If my subsequent conscience is to agree with the preceding one, then the first must have truth as its foundation; only then do I say correctly, "this is good and that is not," or conversely, "this is false and that is correct." From this it is easy to see that a correct, good, and complete **conscience** presupposes truth, **freedom**, and a clear **knowledge** <term>Erkenntniß: in 18th-century philosophy, this refers to 'cognition' or the active mental grasping of a concept</term> of the good. My **doubts** have thereby [developed] regarding the accepted—

->Freemason Lodge in Berlin. 207<-

and how many false conclusions have not entered the world in this manner? How must I avoid this deception? Through clear knowledge <term>Erkenntniß: In Enlightenment philosophy, this refers to the systematic process of attaining certain understanding.</term> and precise investigation of the connection between the source and that which is supposed to have arisen from it. ### §. 12.

<margin>Doubts regarding the secrecy of the Order are presented.</margin>
My doubts reveal themselves in the second question, which arises quite clearly from the Order's principles and is as follows:

Can a society, which has laid the <term>Law of Nature: A system of ethics and justice held to be common to all humans and derivable from nature and reason, rather than from divine revelation or positive law.</term> as the foundation of its association, and set the <term>Happiness: In Enlightenment philosophy, "Glückseligkeit" (felicity or blessedness) was considered the ultimate rational goal of human existence and social organization.</term> of mankind as its final goal, keep the organization aimed toward that end secret?

Everyone who notices what I have set down regarding the Masonic society, and simultaneously pays attention to the Freemasons, will find that the Order answers this question in the affirmative through both words and deeds. I doubt this. Why? Because, first of all, I do not understand how one can lead someone to a goal without means. The law of nature makes me capable of preserving my happiness. This is what the...

—I am convinced of the necessity of secrecy in many institutions when disclosure would harm the country, state, or society, and when making it public could serve no purpose. However, I cannot imagine such a thing regarding **Freemasonry**, because it has derived the provisions of its laws from the laws of nature, virtue, and **philanthropy** <note>original: "Menschenliebe"; a central Enlightenment concept referring to a general love for all of humanity and the desire to promote their well-being.</note>, and seeks to make people happy. All of this cannot possibly be [harmful] in its disclosure; on the contrary, it may be harmful in its withholding. Therefore, according to the law of nature, I can demand the disclosure of a secret from its possessor—provided it aims toward those goals, and he can reveal it without harm to himself, or has already spoken of it, and if knowing it would be beneficial to me. If he withholds it from me, I consider myself entitled to be uneasy and doubtful about it, and to believe that he harbors a mistrust even toward his own institution.

§. 13.

### §. 13.

<margin>The doubts regarding the admission of all religions into the Order are presented.</margin>I come to the investigation of a new question, which is founded in the constitutions of the **Masonic Order** <term>Freymäurerorden; the fraternal organization of Freemasonry</term> and which I cannot, because of certain doubts, accept and approve as the Order does. The question is this:

->Can the Masonic Order, according to its principles, if it truly wishes to practice them, admit all religions and establish among them a true brotherhood and union of hearts?<-

To properly understand this question, it will be necessary to clarify the particular concepts contained within it. Admitting all religions into one society can mean nothing else than leaving the manner and way in which one wishes to serve **God** <note>original: "GOtt"; the author uses capitalized letters for the deity, common in 18th-century German pietistic and philosophical writing to show reverence.</note> to the discretion <note>original: "Willkühr"; in this context, it refers to the exercise of individual free will or personal choice rather than "arbitrariness."</note> of each member. Consequently, the Order does not concern itself with whether one [serves] God as a

<vocab>Masonic Order, Religions, Brotherhood, Union of Hearts, God</vocab>

<summary>The author begins an investigation into whether Freemasonry can truly foster a "union of hearts" while admitting members of all religious faiths. He suggests that such inclusivity implies the Order remains indifferent to the specific religious practices and doctrines of its individual members.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, religious tolerance, pluralism, brotherhood, Enlightenment philosophy, Union of Hearts</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---

<header>Freemasons’ Lodge in Berlin.</header>

Heathen, or Muslim <note>original: "Muselmann"</note>, or Jew, or Christian serves. To its conduct, it is indifferent how its members arrange their internal and external actions for the glorification of the Most High. An idolater can retain his idolatry outside of the Order. The Turk <note>A common 18th-century European term for a Muslim.</note> can remain with his Quran <note>original: "Alcoran"</note>. The Jew can revere the tenets of his Talmud, and the many sects of Christians can believe their incorrect and false opinions—which they hold to be good according to their delusion—to be correct according to their imagination. The purity of Freemasonry is not corrupted by these impure ways of serving **God** <note>original: "GOtt"</note>. It retains its form and beauty in its members, even if those same members, according to their religion outside of the Order, appear ever so ugly, abominable, and deformed. 

What does the concept of a **true brotherhood** and **union of hearts** encompass? The hearts of many unite when they choose a common rule <term>Vorschrift; a prescribed set of instructions or a mandate for conduct</term> by which to organize their free actions. They take—

### Freemason’s Lodge in Berlin.

—union <insert>of hearts</insert> <note>original: "zensvereinigung"; completing the word from the previous page</note> which has been implanted.

# Freemason Lodge in Berlin.

holds it to be true or not, whether out of conviction or imagination. If the latter is the case, then one is merely playing with the word **Religion** and using it for one's own external motives. This conflicts with the **character** of an honest man. A rational and wise man recognizes that the service of the infinite **God** must not be used as a cloak for his deceptions. Can there be a more brazen wickedness than to ensnare others under the guise of serving eternal love <note>The author uses "eternal love" as a title for God, reflecting the Enlightenment-era emphasis on a benevolent Creator</note>, while playing the hypocrite both inside or outside the temples <note>A reference to Masonic lodges, which members often refer to as "temples" of humanity or virtue</note>? 

Certainly, anyone who can be so bold as to manipulate the name, service, and glory of the adorable Being for his own ends—acting now in this way, now in another—is predisposed toward the most base actions and is a disgrace to all rational creatures. Since the **members of the Order** must be honest people, such behavior cannot be suspected of them; I must therefore say: everyone is convinced that his religion is true and to the highest **God**

<margin>Doubt concerning the question: Whether the female sex can be excluded from the **Masonic society** <term>Freymäurergesellschaft</term>?</margin>

Can the fair sex <note>A common 18th-century idiom for women.</note>, according to the **law of nature** <term>Gesetz der Natur</term>, be permanently excluded from a society that seeks and promotes the **happiness** <term>Glückseeligkeit</term> <note>original: "Glückseeligkeit" — a key Enlightenment concept referring to a state of spiritual, moral, and social well-being.</note> of mankind as its primary aim, without any hope of ever holding a seat within it? I have weighed the already disclosed motivations <term>Bewegungsgründe</term> against the tenets <term>Lehrsätze</term> of the Order, examined them, and must confess that they have not convinced me. This slight stubbornness <term>Hartnäckigkeit</term> was instilled in me by a lady of noble birth and great intellect through the arguments she made on behalf of her sex when she spoke with me on the subject. She spoke: "The first motivation is based on the rejection of all suspicion and apprehension regarding certain excesses <term>Ueppigkeiten</term> <note>original: "Ueppigkeiten" — in this context, the term refers to moral impropriety or carnal excesses. It is believed that if women had been granted entry to the assemblies, malice and slander <term>Verleumdung</term> would have had the greatest provocation to run riot as they pleased. How is it possible to believe such a thing, if several ladies were present at once?"

<meta>This page continues the author’s examination of the legalistic arguments against women entering Freemasonry. <page-num>243</page-num>

->### Masonic Lodge in Berlin.<-

...[ranks] of the **female sex** <term>weiblichen Ordens</term> <note>Literally "female order," here referring to the collective status or class of women.</note> and has endeavored to show that women could never provide assurance of their **freedom**; indeed, he has cited **religion** and the **law** <term>die Rechte</term> as evidence for his discourse. It is true that the man is the head of the **marriage** and that dominion—that is, the power to command—is due to him. However, it does not follow from this that he remains an unrestricted and entirely **free person** within that marriage.

In the state of matrimony, he is only the first part among those who have bound themselves to one another. The **welfare** <term>Wolfahrt</term> of the marriage restricts all his actions and commands him, just as it does the wife, to refrain from that which is contrary to their **marital happiness** <term>ehligen Glückseeligkeit</term>, and to do that which can be conducive to it. Consequently, the man is not a **free being** <note>The author is making a radical point: if women are excluded from Freemasonry because they are not "free" (due to marital obligations), then by the same logic, married men are also not truly "free" and should be excluded as well.</note> and has his rights and obligations just like the wife; who, in this state, has left him nothing more than the power to command that which is good for the marriage. If one thinks that this command of his can be arranged according to his **arbitrary will** <term>Willkühr</term>, and

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument from page 243, which challenges the idea that a husband’s authority over his wife is absolute. -># Circular Letter to the<-

...that he could rule his house solely by his absolute power? If one believes this, one is greatly mistaken; for if he, as a rational man, wishes to exercise his **authority** <term>Herrschaft</term>, he must direct it according to the purpose of the **state of matrimony** <term>Ehestand</term>. He must command nothing other than what serves the true best interest of the marriage, and to that extent, a sensible wife accepts his command, recognizes the power of the husband, and is bound to **obedience** <term>Gehorsam</term> toward him. 

What strange consequences would arise if one were to grant the husband unrestricted rule in the state of matrimony? From this limitation of the husband's power flows his duty to follow and obey the advice and will of his wife—when it aims at the **happiness** <term>Glückseeligkeit</term> <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "happiness" (Glückseligkeit) was often seen as the ultimate goal of rational social contracts, including marriage.</note> of the **marital society** <term>ehlichen Gesellschaft</term>—just as much as it is her obligation to submit to the will of the **husband** <term>Eheherren</term> <note>Literally "Marriage-Lord" or master of the house.</note>. From this it follows:

1) A rational man never misuses his authority to the detriment of the

<summary>The author argues that a husband's authority in marriage is not absolute but limited by the goal of mutual marital happiness. He asserts that a husband must listen to and "obey" a wife's sensible advice, just as she obeys him, establishing marriage as a reciprocal social contract.</summary>

<keywords>marriage, male authority, obedience, marital happiness, gender roles, social contract, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

of marriage, and he would do so if he wished to prevent the woman, through a mere decree of power, from that which could make her modest, rational, truth-loving, **virtuous** <term>tugendhaft</term>, and blissful.

2) A man must never imagine himself to be such a being in his marriage who can do whatever he wishes; rather, through the **wise arrangement** <term>weise Einrichtung</term> of his marital conduct, he must always strive, as a free head, to choose what is best. As a member of the **marital society** <term>ehligen Gesellschaft</term> restricted by the bonds of marriage, he must fulfill precisely what pertains to his own prosperity, that of his wife, and his entire **domestic state** <term>häußlichen Standes</term>.

### §. 15.

<margin>
Doubts concerning the ideas of Freemasonry, regarding the right of the high authority over the members of the lodge.
</margin>
I must investigate the final question, which relates to the well-known organization of the Freemason Order, as I harbor some doubts regarding it. It reads:

> Can the sovereign prince <note>original: "Landesfürst" — the ruler of a specific German state or territory.</note> or the ordinary supreme authority of a land rightfully demand the disclosure of the mysterious organization of the Freemason Order without joining the Order; and can he release his subject, who is a Freemason, from the oath of secrecy <term>Eyd der Verschwiegenheit: a solemn promise made by Masons to never reveal the rituals or secrets of the group.</term> sworn, in order to reveal the secret?

The Order denies this question in both word and deed, and for that purpose has taken upon itself the decision regarding the doubts arising against it; however, since these decisions [do] not yet <note>The sentence is interrupted here as the page ends; the catchword "völlig" (completely) suggests the next page begins with "completely satisfy me."</note>

### §. 15.

Doubts concerning the ideas of Freemasonry, regarding the right of the high sovereign authority over the lodge members.

> Can the **sovereign prince** <term>Landesfürst: the territorial ruler of a German state.</term> or the regular supreme authority of a country rightfully demand the revelation of the mysterious organization of the Order of Freemasons without joining the Order; and can they release their **subject** <term>Unterthan: a person living under the rule of a monarch, owing them loyalty and obedience.</term>, who is a Freemason, from the **oath of secrecy** <note>original: "Eyde der Verschwiegenheit" — the binding promise of silence taken by initiates into the Masonic order.</note> taken, in order to discover the secret?

The Order denies this question in both word and deed, and has for that purpose undertaken the resolution of the doubts that have arisen against it; however, since these resolutions have not yet

<page-num>271</page-num>
<header>Freemason Lodge in Berlin. 271</header>

<note>This sentence begins on the previous page.</note> ...obligated by opportunity to establish or admit this society, which is so beneficial to their country. Such a society is brought into reality either by **native-born** or **naturalized subjects** <term>Eingebohrne oder angenommene Unterthanen: 'Native-born' refers to those born within the territory, while 'naturalized' refers to foreign-born individuals who have been granted the legal status of a subject.</term>. Whether it arises from one or the other, or both at once, all its members henceforth become **subjects** <term>Unterthanen: individuals legally bound to the rule of a sovereign.</term> of the land through this, their collective establishment or admission. Beyond their general obligations, they acquire a specific duty to accept not only the laws of the land in general, but also to receive the laws of their own society from the **sovereign** <term>Landesherr: the lord or ruler of the territory.</term>. I do not deny that a society wishing to unite for the benefit of a country can draft its own collective laws and bind itself to them with or without an **oath**; however, I deny that they can do this without the knowledge of the **head of state** <term>Oberhaupt: the supreme leader or monarch.</term>. For in doing so, the society acts against its duty as subjects and withdraws from the **sovereign prince** <term>Landesfürst: the prince or hereditary ruler.</term> the right that belongs to him: to use his **freedom** <note>In 18th-century legal terms, 'freedom' here refers to the ruler's sovereign prerogative or discretionary power.</note> to shape their actions and omissions. When such a society takes an **oath of loyalty and secrecy** from its members, does it demand it through its own inherent power? By no means; rather, it demands it by virtue of the **subordinate authority** <term>Untergewalt: power delegated by a higher authority to a specific group or institution.</term> transferred to it, which derives its influence from the **superior authority** <term>Obergewalt: the supreme power of the sovereign state.</term> that approved its establishment.

To illustrate this, let us consider the **guilds, trade corporations, and associations** <term>Innungen und Zünfte: traditional organizations of craftsmen and merchants that held specific legal rights, often including the right to swear in members.</term> that exist within a **commonwealth** <term>gemeines Wesen: the state or organized public community.</term>. When these demand and actually receive an oath from their members, does this occur without the prior knowledge of the **magistracy** <term>Obrigkeit: the governing body or administrative authorities.</term>? Who would admit such a thing? No one, except he who fails to consider that they derive their power from the high authorities.

<header>Freemason Lodge in Berlin.</header>

...set up an assembly and make arrangements in the father’s house without the father’s prior knowledge? <note>The author continues a metaphor where the "father" is the Sovereign King and the "son" is a subject or a Freemason.</note> Can he use a room in his father’s dwelling for this purpose without giving an account of it to the father? Can the father be satisfied with the son’s excuse when he asks about it, by saying: a father must not judge the thoughts of his child? <note>This refers to the legal principle that "thoughts are free" (*cogitationis poenam nemo patitur*), which the author argues does not apply to organized meetings.</note> And can the father finally be appeased by this answer and remain silent, while the son nevertheless makes his assembly known to many others?

->§. 16.<-

<margin>Conclusion of the Circular Letter.</margin>
This is that which troubles me regarding the Honorable **Order** <term>Orden: here referring to the organized body of Freemasonry.</term> of the Freemasons. I hope that in the presentation of these thoughts of mine, I have proceeded sincerely, honestly, and respectfully. Only malice, which seeks to poison everything, will accuse me of a deviation from the paths of love and esteem for the Order. I hope, however, the Order will [defend] <note>original: "verthei=" — a catchword completing as "vertheidigen" (defend) on the next page.</note> me against these accusations...

<meta>catchword: [defend]</meta>

<summary>The author concludes his argument by using a domestic analogy of a father and son to illustrate that a ruler has a right to know what happens within his borders, finally transitioning to a respectful closing statement regarding his intentions toward the Masonic Order.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, sovereignty, rule of law, secrecy, Berlin, political philosophy, social hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 283, where the author expressed hope that the Freemasonic Order would defend him against accusations of malice. He concludes his formal address by emphasizing his humility and his desire for a rational resolution to his doubts.</meta>

<page-num>284</page-num>
<header>Epistle to the...</header>

defend. For I testify before God and the world that I have been driven to this **circular letter** <term>Sendschreiben: A formal public letter or epistle intended to provoke discussion on a specific topic.</term> of mine only by a love for the truth and a hatred for premature judgment. I am not at all proud or stubborn in my **reasons for doubt** <note>The author is referring to the legal and moral objections he raised in the preceding sections regarding the secrecy of the Lodge.</note>; therefore, I submit them to the wise resolution and judgment of the **Order** <term>Orden: Referring to the Freemasons, framed here as a structured, quasi-knightly society.</term>, and I also believe that their decision will be as easy for others as it is difficult for me. In this confidence, which holds the **Order** in high esteem, I surrender this modest essay to the truth and to those who love it, that is, to you

->Venerable Gentlemen,<-

->to whom I shall remain bound with the most sincere respect without ceasing,<-

->Written at Göttingen<-
->on March 24, 1744.<-

Your most obliged servant,
the Author.

<summary>The author concludes his epistle by submitting his critiques to the judgment of the Masonic Order, asserting that his doubts arise from a sincere love of truth rather than personal animosity. He signs off from the university town of Göttingen, a center of Enlightenment thought, in the spring of 1744.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, Freemasonry, Göttingen, 1744, academic dissent, civil obedience, circular letter</keywords>
